  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_1/projects/cost-verify-agent-rl/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$.",
    "newString": "Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$ (our experiments use a penalty variant with $r_- = -0.1$ per invalid action; Section~\\ref{sec:setup}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation and GRPO}\n\\label{sec:problem_formulation}\n\nWe model the multi-turn agent as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) where the policy $\\pi_\\phi(a_t \\mid h_t)$ generates actions conditioned on interaction history $h_t = (o_1, a_1, \\ldots, o_t)$. Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$. GRPO~\\cite{shao2024deepseekmath} samples $K$ rollouts per task and computes group-relative advantages:\n\\begin{align}\n\\hat{A}_k &= \\frac{R_k - \\mu}{\\sigma + \\epsilon}, \\notag \\\\\n\\mu &= \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{j=1}^K R_j, \\quad \\sigma = \\sqrt{\\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{j=1}^K (R_j - \\mu)^2}\n\\label{eq:grpo_advantage}\n\\end{align}\nGRPO is episode-level by design: each rollout receives one return and one advantage, with no inherent temporal discounting.\n\n\\subsection{Step-Independent Windowing}\n\\label{sec:step_independent}\n\nAgent RL frameworks must reconcile GRPO's episode-level design with the memory constraints of long trajectories. RAGEN~\\cite{ragen2025} introduces \\textbf{step-independent training}: the trajectory is partitioned into non-overlapping $h$-step windows, each receiving a discounted return:\n\\begin{equation}\nR_k = \\gamma^{(N - k)} \\cdot R_{\\text{episode}}\n\\label{eq:step_return}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $N = \\lceil T/h \\rceil$ is the number of windows. Each window is optimized as an independent GRPO sample. This reduces sequence length from $O(T)$ to $O(h)$ and multiplies the effective batch size, but structurally alters credit assignment.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Step-independent windowing (left) partitions the trajectory into fixed windows, each optimized independently. This introduces two failure mechanisms: M1 (information loss) truncates context at window boundaries, and M2 (sign misattribution) assigns all windows the same advantage sign based on episode outcome. Episode-level training (right) treats the full trajectory as a single sample, eliminating both mechanisms.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Dual Mechanism Analysis}\n\\label{sec:dual_mechanism}\n\nTwo mechanisms jointly cause training failure under step-independent optimization.\n\n\\subsubsection{Mechanism 1: Policy Information Loss}\n\\label{sec:m1_information_loss}\n\nStep-independent training truncates context at both inference and training time. The policy $\\pi_\\phi(a_t \\mid h_{\\text{window}_k})$ conditions on only the local $h$-step history, losing access to earlier observations. With $h{=}2$, a window at step~8 has no access to the outcome of an exploration action at step~4, so the policy cannot learn strategies spanning more than $h$ steps. This imposes a \\textit{constant} performance ceiling: no amount of optimization can recover absent information.\n\n\\subsubsection{Mechanism 2: Advantage Sign Misattribution}\n\\label{sec:m2_sign_misattribution}\n\nGroup normalization under step-independent training produces advantages whose sign reflects the \\textit{episode} outcome, not local action quality:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\text{sign}(\\hat{A}_k) \\approx \\text{sign}(R_{\\text{episode}} - \\mu)\n\\label{eq:sign_correlation}\n\\end{equation}\nIn failed episodes ($R_{\\text{episode}} = 0$), all windows receive negative advantages when $\\mu > 0$, including windows with locally correct actions. Conversely, successful episodes reinforce all windows, including those with suboptimal actions. Unlike M1's constant ceiling, sign misattribution is \\textit{cumulative}: each wrong-sign gradient step further erodes policy competence. The discount factor $\\gamma^{(N-k)}$ introduces magnitude decay across positions, but sign errors are strictly more harmful: magnitude decay slows convergence, while sign errors cause active divergence.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Training}\n\\label{sec:episode_level}\n\nEpisode-level training addresses both mechanisms by treating the complete trajectory as a single GRPO sample. The full history $h_t$ is available at every step (eliminating M1), and a single advantage $\\hat{A}$ applies uniformly to all tokens (eliminating M2). Uniform reinforcement of successful trajectories preserves good sub-strategies; window-level sign errors actively destroy them. This is GRPO's original design; step-independent windowing is the deviation.\n\n\\subsection{Factorial Experiment Design}\n\\label{sec:factorial_design}\n\nTo separate the two mechanisms, we implement a $2 \\times 2$ factorial varying observation scope and advantage granularity: \\textbf{Cell~A} (truncated $h{=}2$ + step-level advantage; both mechanisms active), \\textbf{Cell~B} (truncated $h{=}2$ + episode-level advantage; only M1 active), and \\textbf{Cell~D} (full trajectory + episode-level advantage; neither active). The fourth cell, \\textbf{Cell~C} (full trajectory + step-level advantage; only M2 active), would isolate sign misattribution without information loss. We attempted this configuration but encountered persistent training instability (Section~\\ref{sec:factorial_experiments}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cell_c}). Comparing A vs.\\ B isolates sign misattribution; comparing B vs.\\ D isolates information loss.\n",

Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$. GRPO~\\cite{shao2024deepseekmath} samples $K$ rollouts per task and computes group-relative advantages:",

Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$ (our experiments use a penalty variant with $r_- = -0.1$ per invalid action; Section~\\ref{sec:setup}). GRPO~\\cite{shao2024deepseekmath} samples $K$ rollouts per task and computes group-relative advantages:",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013ezAfYuqeB6xkah9RYxxj9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	Step-independent training collapses while episode-level training succeeds; a factorial design separates the two mechanisms; and window size $h$ modulates severity.
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We evaluate on ALFWorld~\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}, a text-based household environment with six task types requiring sequential reasoning over up to 50 steps. The environment provides ternary reward: $R \in \{r_-, 0, r_+\}$, where $r_- = -0.1$ per invalid action, $0$ for valid but unsuccessful actions, and $r_+$ for task success. Our theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:theory}) assumes the simplified binary case ($R \in \{0, r_+\}$), which yields an upper bound on sign misattribution severity; Section~\ref{sec:sign_verification} validates this bound under both reward structures. The base policy is Qwen3-1.7B fine-tuned on expert demonstrations (SFT baseline: 12.5\% in-distribution). At this baseline success rate, Proposition~2 predicts the majority of gradient updates carry incorrect sign. All conditions use RAGEN~\cite{ragen2025} (v0.1, commit \texttt{3a6b48b})\footnote{Our implementation extends RAGEN v0.1 with episode-level training support. Code available upon acceptance.} with GRPO~\cite{shao2024deepseekmath} ($K{=}8$; full hyperparameters in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparameters}). We report success rate on a 134-task OOD set (primary) and 32-task in-distribution set (secondary). Seed counts are noted per condition.
10	
11	\subsection{Factorial Mechanism Separation}
12	\label{sec:factorial_experiments}
13	
14	We implement the factorial design from Section~\ref{sec:factorial_design} with two seeds per condition. Cell~C (full observation, step-level advantage) would isolate M2 without M1, but we encountered persistent training instability in this configuration (Appendix~\ref{app:cell_c}). Cell~C's NaN divergence, qualitatively different from Cell~A's gradual collapse, suggests that information loss may have an unintended stabilizing effect on gradient variance, and that the M1--M2 interaction may be non-additive (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
15	
16	\subsection{Results}
17	\label{sec:results}
18	
19	Cell~A collapses in two phases (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}): task success drops to 0\% while action validity initially remains high, then continued sign misattribution destroys even basic formatting.% exp_id: hsweep_h2_seed1, hsweep_h2_seed2
20	Cell~B eliminates sign misattribution while retaining information loss: training success improves, but OOD generalization is bounded by the information-loss ceiling. Both seeds converge to the same OOD ceiling despite different training trajectories, confirming the bottleneck is architectural rather than statistical.% exp_id: factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed1, factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed2
21	
22	Cell~D is the only condition that substantially exceeds the SFT baseline, with monotonic OOD improvement across both seeds.% exp_id: exp029_episode_level, exp029_seed3_ood_v2
23	
24	The A$\to$B comparison isolates M2: eliminating sign misattribution converts catastrophic collapse into bounded learning. The B$\to$D comparison isolates M1: removing the information-loss ceiling enables OOD generalization. Both mechanisms are necessary to explain the full failure pattern. Figure~\ref{fig:hsweep}(a) visualizes these trajectories.
25	
26	\begin{figure*}[t]
27	\centering
28	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_training_dynamics.pdf}
29	\caption{(a)~Training dynamics across factorial conditions (metric type indicated in legend). Cell~A collapses within 5 steps; Cell~B shows bounded learning; Cell~D improves monotonically. (b)~Window size $h$ vs.\ success rate (seed~1). $h{\leq}20$ bars use a 32-task in-distribution eval set; the episode-level bar uses a 134-task OOD set (dotted separator); magnitudes are not directly comparable across eval sets. A credit assignment threshold between $h{=}10$ and $h{=}20$ separates collapse from recovery. Hatched bars indicate collapsed conditions.}
30	\label{fig:hsweep}
31	\end{figure*}
32	
33	\begin{table}[t]
34	\centering
35	\caption{1.7B results on ALFWorld (best checkpoint per seed, evaluated on held-out test sets). In-Dist: 32-task set; OOD: 134-task set. Cell~A reports best seed (2 seeds); Cell~B reports best checkpoint at step~5 (2 seeds). Cell~D reports mean of 2 seeds (In-Dist range: 28.1--40.6; OOD range: 10.4--23.9); Cell~D results are consistent with the 11-seed evaluation (Table~\ref{tab:seed_variability}), where mean OOD$=$17.2\% with 36\% generalization rate. Dense reward and REINFORCE++: 1 seed each.}
36	\label{tab:main_results}
37	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
38	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
39	\toprule
40	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Mechanisms} & \textbf{In-Dist (\%)} & \textbf{OOD (\%)} \\
41	\midrule
42	SFT baseline & --- & 12.50 & 6.72 \\
43	Cell A ($h{=}2$, step) & M1+M2 & 0 & 0 \\
44	Cell B ($h{=}2$, episode) & M1 only & 18.75 & 5.97 \\
45	Cell D (full, episode) & neither & \textbf{34.38} & \textbf{17.16} \\
46	\midrule
47	Dense reward & M1+M2 & 0 & 0 \\
48	REINFORCE++ & neither & 12.50 & --- \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}}
51	% exp_id: hsweep_h2_seed1, factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed1, exp029_episode_level, exp029_seed3_v2, exp013f, gae_baseline_seed1
52	\end{table}
53	
54	\paragraph{Additional conditions.} Dense reward, REINFORCE++, and discount-factor sweep experiments all confirm that sign misattribution, not reward sparsity or discounting, drives the failure (Appendix~\ref{app:additional}).
55	
56	\paragraph{Window-size sweep.} Varying window size reveals a credit assignment threshold between $h{=}10$ and $h{=}20$ that separates collapse from recovery (Figure~\ref{fig:hsweep}b; seed~1). Below this threshold, all conditions collapse to 0\%; above it, success rate increases monotonically with window size. This threshold is observed in a single seed; multi-seed verification is needed to confirm its robustness.% exp_id: h_sweep_series, exp029_seed1
57	\label{sec:results_hsweep}
58	
59	\subsection{Sign Misattribution Verification}
60	\label{sec:sign_verification}
61	
62	We distinguish three metrics that each capture a different aspect of credit assignment quality:
63	
64	\textit{(1) Sign-episode correlation}: whether advantage sign tracks episode outcome (success/failure). Under pure binary reward (penalty${=}0$), this correlation is 100\% ($n{=}157$ windows; Figure~\ref{fig:sign_misattribution}), directly confirming M2: advantage sign is entirely determined by episode outcome, not local action quality. This is by construction, since all windows in a successful episode share the same positive return under binary reward.% exp_id: binary_credit_agreement_iter0
65	
66	\textit{(2) Credit agreement rate}: whether advantage sign matches local action quality, operationalized through action validity as a proxy for local correctness (Definition~1). Under step-independent training with penalty reward ($h{=}2$, $n{=}3{,}000$ windows), only 52.07\% of windows with valid actions receive positive advantage, marginally above random chance ($p{=}0.025$ vs.\ 50\%; $+$2.07pp effect size). This near-random credit agreement shows that advantage sign carries almost no information about local action quality. Penalty reward partially breaks sign-episode correlation by introducing within-group variance, but the resulting signal remains too weak for meaningful credit assignment. This operationalization is conservative: successful episodes may contain suboptimal windows, making our validity label an upper bound on true local correctness. Even under this generous criterion, credit agreement remains near-random, strengthening the diagnosis.% exp_id: 7b_cell_a_h2
67	
68	\textit{(3) $P(\text{correct} \mid \text{update})$}: the theoretical upper bound from Proposition~2 ($\leq$19.1\% at $p{=}12.5\%$, $K{=}8$). This bounds the fraction of non-zero gradient updates with correct sign, a different quantity from the credit agreement rate, which conditions on action validity rather than gradient participation. The two metrics are complementary: Proposition~2 bounds worst-case gradient quality; credit agreement measures empirical signal strength.
69	
70	Combined with $\gamma{=}1.0$ collapse (Appendix~\ref{app:additional}), these measurements confirm sign misattribution as the causal driver of training failure.
71	
72	\begin{figure}[t]
73	\centering
74	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_sign_misattribution.pdf}
75	\caption{Sign-episode correlation (binary reward, penalty${=}0$) vs.\ credit agreement rate (penalty reward). Under binary reward, advantage sign perfectly tracks episode outcome (100\%, $n{=}157$), confirming M2. Under penalty reward, credit agreement with local action quality drops to 52.1\% ($n{=}3{,}000$), near random chance (dashed line). These are distinct metrics: the former measures episode-level sign correlation, the latter measures local credit assignment quality.}
76	\label{fig:sign_misattribution}
77	\end{figure}

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nStep-independent training collapses while episode-level training succeeds; a factorial design separates the two mechanisms; and window size $h$ modulates severity.\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe evaluate on ALFWorld~\\cite{shridhar2021alfworld}, a text-based household environment with six task types requiring sequential reasoning over up to 50 steps. The environment provides ternary reward: $R \\in \\{r_-, 0, r_+\\}$, where $r_- = -0.1$ per invalid action, $0$ for valid but unsuccessful actions, and $r_+$ for task success. Our theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:theory}) assumes the simplified binary case ($R \\in \\{0, r_+\\}$), which yields an upper bound on sign misattribution severity; Section~\\ref{sec:sign_verification} validates this bound under both reward structures. All conditions use RAGEN~\\cite{ragen2025} (v0.1, commit \\texttt{3a6b48b})\\footnote{Our implementation extends RAGEN v0.1 with episode-level training support. We report success rate on a 134-task OOD set (primary) and 32-task in-distribution set (secondary). Seed counts are noted per condition.\n\n\\subsection{Factorial Mechanism Separation}\n\\label{sec:factorial_experiments}\n\nWe implement the factorial design from Section~\\ref{sec:factorial_design} with two seeds per condition. Cell~C (full observation, step-level advantage) would isolate M2 without M1, but we encountered persistent training instability in this configuration (Appendix~\\ref{app:cell_c}). Cell~C's NaN divergence, qualitatively different from Cell~A's gradual collapse, suggests that information loss may have an unintended stabilizing effect on gradient variance, and that the M1--M2 interaction may be non-additive (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nCell~A collapses in two phases (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}): task success drops to 0\\% while action validity initially remains high, then continued sign misattribution destroys even basic formatting.% exp_id: hsweep_h2_seed1, hsweep_h2_seed2\nCell~B eliminates sign misattribution while retaining information loss: training success improves, but OOD generalization is bounded by the information-loss ceiling. Both seeds converge to the same OOD ceiling despite different training trajectories, confirming the bottleneck is architectural rather than statistical.% exp_id: factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed1, factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed2\n\nCell~D is the only condition that substantially exceeds the SFT baseline, with monotonic OOD improvement across both seeds.% exp_id: exp029_episode_level, exp029_seed3_ood_v2\n\nThe A$\\to$B comparison isolates M2: eliminating sign misattribution converts catastrophic collapse into bounded learning. The B$\\to$D comparison isolates M1: removing the information-loss ceiling enables OOD generalization. Both mechanisms are necessary to explain the full failure pattern. Figure~\\ref{fig:hsweep}(a) visualizes these trajectories.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_training_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{(a)~Training dynamics across factorial conditions (metric type indicated in legend). Cell~A collapses within 5 steps; Cell~B shows bounded learning; Cell~D improves monotonically. (b)~Window size $h$ vs.\\ success rate (seed~1). $h{\\leq}20$ bars use a 32-task in-distribution eval set; the episode-level bar uses a 134-task OOD set (dotted separator); magnitudes are not directly comparable across eval sets. A credit assignment threshold between $h{=}10$ and $h{=}20$ separates collapse from recovery. Hatched bars indicate collapsed conditions.}\n\\label{fig:hsweep}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{1.7B results on ALFWorld (best checkpoint per seed, evaluated on held-out test sets). In-Dist: 32-task set; OOD: 134-task set. Cell~A reports best seed (2 seeds); Cell~B reports best checkpoint at step~5 (2 seeds). Cell~D reports mean of 2 seeds (In-Dist range: 28.1--40.6; OOD range: 10.4--23.9); Cell~D results are consistent with the 11-seed evaluation (Table~\\ref{tab:seed_variability}), where mean OOD$=$17.2\\% with 36\\% generalization rate. Dense reward and REINFORCE++: 1 seed each.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Mechanisms} & \\textbf{In-Dist (\\%)} & \\textbf{OOD (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSFT baseline & --- & 12.50 & 6.72 \\\\\nCell A ($h{=}2$, step) & M1+M2 & 0 & 0 \\\\\nCell B ($h{=}2$, episode) & M1 only & 18.75 & 5.97 \\\\\nCell D (full, episode) & neither & \\textbf{34.38} & \\textbf{17.16} \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense reward & M1+M2 & 0 & 0 \\\\\nREINFORCE++ & neither & 12.50 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n% exp_id: hsweep_h2_seed1, factorial_h2_episodeadv_seed1, exp029_episode_level, exp029_seed3_v2, exp013f, gae_baseline_seed1\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Additional conditions.} Dense reward, REINFORCE++, and discount-factor sweep experiments all confirm that sign misattribution, not reward sparsity or discounting, drives the failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n\\paragraph{Window-size sweep.} Varying window size reveals a credit assignment threshold between $h{=}10$ and $h{=}20$ that separates collapse from recovery (Figure~\\ref{fig:hsweep}b; seed~1). Below this threshold, all conditions collapse to 0\\%; above it, success rate increases monotonically with window size. The two metrics are complementary: Proposition~2 bounds worst-case gradient quality; credit agreement measures empirical signal strength.\n\nCombined with $\\gamma{=}1.0$ collapse (Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}), these measurements confirm sign misattribution as the causal driver of training failure.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_sign_misattribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Sign-episode correlation (binary reward, penalty${=}0$) vs.\\ credit agreement rate (penalty reward). ```text
1	\section{Theoretical Analysis}
2	\label{sec:theory}
3	
4	Sign misattribution is the more fundamental failure mechanism because it causes active divergence, not merely slow convergence. We derive the probability of incorrect gradient direction below; full proofs appear in Appendix~\ref{app:theory_full}.
5	
6	\textbf{Definition 1} (Locally correct action). \textit{An action $a_t$ is \textbf{locally correct} if it would receive positive advantage under an oracle that observes the full episode history $h_t$ and has access to the true action-value function. Equivalently, window $k$ contains locally correct actions if replacing them (holding all other windows fixed) would decrease the probability of episode success.}
7	
8	\textbf{Proposition 1.} \textit{Under step-independent training with binary episode reward $R_{\text{episode}} \in \{0, r_+\}$, the probability that window $k$'s advantage $\hat{A}_k$ has the correct sign (aligned with local action quality) approaches the base success rate $p$ as the number of rollouts $K$ per group increases.}
9	
10	\textit{Proof sketch.} In a failed episode ($R_{\text{episode}} = 0$), all windows receive $R_k = 0$. If any rollout in the group succeeded, $\mu > 0$, so every window in the failed episode receives $\hat{A}_k < 0$---including windows with locally correct actions. The probability of receiving the wrong sign in a failed episode is $1 - (1-p)^{K-1}$ (at least one group member succeeded). As $K$ grows, this approaches $1-p$, and the overall probability of correct sign approaches $p$. \hfill$\square$
11	
12	The key distinction is temporal. Information loss (M1) imposes a constant ceiling determined by $h$; sign misattribution (M2) is cumulative---each wrong-sign gradient step erodes policy competence below that ceiling. A wrong-sign gradient moves the optimizer \textit{away} from good policies; a reduced-magnitude gradient from discounting ($\gamma^{(N-k)}$) still points toward good policies. Sign errors produce active divergence; magnitude errors merely slow convergence (Appendix~\ref{app:sign_fundamental}).
13	
14	\textbf{Proposition 2.}\label{prop:correct_sign} \textit{Let $p$ denote the episode success rate and $K$ the number of rollouts per group. Under step-independent training with binary episode reward, the fraction of gradient updates with correct sign is upper bounded by:}
15	\begin{equation}
16	P(\text{correct} \mid \text{update}) \leq \frac{p}{1 - (1-p)^{K}}
17	\label{eq:correct_conditional}
18	\end{equation}
19	
20	\textit{Derivation.} Three mutually exclusive cases determine gradient direction for a window with locally correct actions: (1)~episode succeeded (probability $p$): correct sign; (2)~episode failed but group contains a success (probability $(1{-}p)[1{-}(1{-}p)^{K-1}]$): wrong sign; (3)~entire group failed (probability $(1{-}p)^K$): zero gradient. Conditioning on non-zero gradient (Cases 1 and 2) yields Equation~\ref{eq:correct_conditional}. The bound is tight when episode success implies all constituent windows are locally correct; in practice, successful episodes may contain suboptimal windows, so the true fraction of correctly signed updates may be lower.
21	
22	At $p{=}12.5\%$, $K{=}8$, this yields an upper bound of 19.1\% on correctly signed gradient updates (Figure~\ref{fig:theory_curve}). In the large-$K$ limit, $P(\text{correct} \mid \text{update}) \to p$: ${\sim}87.5\%$ of updates for locally correct windows point in the wrong direction.
23	
24	\begin{figure}[t]
25	\centering
26	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_theory_curve.pdf}
27	\caption{Upper bound on the probability of correct gradient sign as a function of episode success rate $p$ (Proposition~2). At typical agent task success rates ($p < 0.2$, shaded), the majority of gradient updates carry incorrect sign regardless of group size $K$. At ALFWorld's baseline ($p{=}12.5\%$, $K{=}8$), at most 19.1\% of updates are correct. The dashed diagonal shows the large-$K$ limit $P_{\text{correct}} \to p$.}
28	\label{fig:theory_curve}
29	\end{figure}
30	
31	\paragraph{Severity scales with task difficulty.} Sign misattribution becomes \textit{more} severe as the task gets harder (Equation~\ref{eq:correct_conditional}): at $p{=}50\%$, $P(\text{correct} \mid \text{update}) \leq 50.2\%$; at $p{=}5\%$, at most 5.1\% of updates are correct. Multi-turn agent tasks with low baseline success rates are precisely where credit assignment matters most, yet where step-independent training produces the worst gradient signal. Increasing $K$ does not help: more updates occur (fewer all-fail groups), but each remains equally likely to carry the wrong sign.
32	
33	\paragraph{Reward saturation.} When all $K$ rollouts receive the same binary reward, $\sigma = 0$ and $\hat{A}_k = 0$, producing zero gradient. At $p{=}12.5\%$, $K{=}8$, the all-fail probability is $(1{-}p)^K \approx 34.4\%$; combined with the negligible all-success case ($p^K < 10^{-7}$), roughly one-third of batches yield no gradient at all. This compounds sign misattribution: of the ${\sim}66\%$ of batches that produce a gradient, at most 19.1\% have the correct sign. The effective learning rate is at most $0.66 \times 0.19 \approx 12.5\%$ of nominal---well below the regime where convergence guarantees hold.
34	
35	\paragraph{Cumulative divergence.} Sign misattribution produces a systematic gradient bias. Let $\theta^*$ denote an optimal policy parameterization. At each update, the expected gradient under step-independent training is:
36	\begin{equation}
37	\mathbb{E}[\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}] = P_c \cdot g_+ + (1 - P_c) \cdot g_-
38	\label{eq:biased_gradient}
39	\end{equation}
40	where $P_c = P(\text{correct} \mid \text{update})$, $g_+$ points toward $\theta^*$, and $g_-$ points away. Empirical measurement from training logs confirms approximately comparable per-group advantage magnitudes ($|\hat{A}_{\text{correct}}|/|\hat{A}_{\text{wrong}}| \approx 1.32$; measured from a single training snapshot; validation across seeds and training stages would strengthen this estimate). When $P_c < 0.5$ (which holds for $p < 0.5$; Equation~\ref{eq:correct_conditional}), the expected gradient points weakly away from good policies. The cumulative effect of this directional bias, amplified by the M1--M2 positive feedback loop (Section~\ref{sec:dual_mechanism}), drives progressive policy degradation: information loss (M1) caps $p$; lower $p$ increases wrong-sign frequency under M2, which further depresses $p$, producing the empirically observed collapse.
41	
```

Episodes of $T$ steps terminate with binary reward $R \in \{0, r_+\}$ (our experiments use a penalty variant with $r_- = -0.1$ per invalid action; Section~\ref{sec:setup}).
